ROUND 141 - H19 assessed: the first clause is a confirmed unfixed defect, and the mechanism is two lines of arithmetic.

I pulled H19's acceptance text from the server contract (`13-server-repair-contract.md:297`) rather than the register's one-line summary, and it is unusually precise:

  "Six requested movement families cannot vanish due to ceil/slice; stored pattern beats category; recent window contains sessions, not last seven individual exercises"
  - evidenced by extending `backend/__tests__/workoutBuilderLongHorizon.test.mjs` and `backend/tests/unit/bootcampGenerationSemantics.test.mjs`.

CLAUSE 1 IS UNFIXED, and the code names itself. `workoutBuilderService.mjs:576` computes `Math.ceil(exerciseCount / movementCategories.length)` — rounding UP — then `:585-594` pushes each family's picks GROUPED BY FAMILY IN ORDER, and `:596` trims with `slice(0, exerciseCount)` FROM THE END. Ask for 6 exercises across 4 movement families and the arithmetic gives `ceil(6/4)=2` → 8 chosen → the first 6 kept → **the fourth family disappears from the day**. Rounding up is what over-fills, and truncating from the end is what drops the LAST families rather than thinning each — which is exactly the clause's wording. This is not a hypothesis: it is the arithmetic, in order, with the rounding direction and the truncation direction both visible.

CLAUSE 3 looks unfixed for the same kind of reason: the rotation window is `recentExerciseKeys.slice(-7)` (`:1245` and `:1266`) — the last seven EXERCISES. The criterion says the window must contain SESSIONS; at six exercises per session this window is barely one session, so "recent" is far shorter than the requirement intends.

CLAUSE 2 is NOT yet settled: the stored pattern is read (`:239`, `:439`, `:717`, and `workoutBuilderCandidateService.mjs:78`), but whether it BEATS a derived category inside `selectExercises` has not been read yet.

NOT IMPLEMENTED, and the reason is the same one that governed H23's discovery round: this row changes GENERATED PLANS. The packet pinned generator behaviour in several tests on purpose, so the slice must begin with RED tests in the two files the contract names and be reconciled against those pinned expectations — not patched at the end of a long session with no room to verify. The disposition row and the receipt now carry the mechanism, the three clause verdicts as they stand, and the exact starting point.
ROUND 144 - H19 clause 1: the fix WORKED, and I reverted it because it moved a pinned requirement I had not accounted for.

WHAT THE RED PROVED, first try once the harness was right: with the recorded context recipe the test ran and failed exactly as the code read predicted —

  expected Set{ 'push', 'pull', 'squat', 'hinge' } to deeply equal Set{ 'push', 'pull', 'squat', …(3) }

At EIGHT exercises across the six `full_body` families, only FOUR families were trained: `lunge` and `core` vanished. The mechanism I had read (ceil over-fills, picks are pushed grouped by family, the final slice truncates from the end) was confirmed by execution rather than inference. The other three assertions passed — count 8, count 6 (an exact multiple) and count 4 — which sharpens the defect to precisely the non-divisible case.

THE FIX WORKED TOO: floor-plus-remainder quotas gave every family its share, the total stayed exactly the requested count, and all four tests went green. It is preserved as evidence, not shipped: `hg370-h19-fix-reverted-keeping-this-as-evidence.mjs`.

WHY IT WAS REVERTED, and this is the part worth carrying forward: the full backend suite went to **1 failed | 10323 passed**, on a test that pins a REQUIREMENT, not a preference —

  `__tests__/workoutBuilderService.intelligence.test.mjs:126` "lets a power phase (NASM 5) opt back into high-impact work"
  expected `ankle_hops` in the selection at `exerciseCount: 4, nasmPhase: 5`; got `['goblet_squat', 'romanian_deadlift']`

The cause is a coupling I had not accounted for when I chose the allocation: **the novelty budget is created ONCE (`workoutBuilderService.mjs:583`) and passed to EVERY `selectExercises` call**, so the number and order of those calls changes how the budget is consumed. My `if (categoryQuota <= 0) continue;` skipped calls that used to happen, and the selection shifted beyond the allocation itself. The criterion is about ALLOCATION, but the blast radius includes budget-coupled SELECTION — and a fix that silently changes which high-impact exercise a NASM-5 power phase may pick is not a fix I am willing to ship without understanding that coupling.

So the change was reverted in full (`git checkout HEAD -- backend/services/workoutBuilderService.mjs`, verified 0 diff against HEAD) and the RED test removed from the suite, because a red test in the tree is worse than a recorded gap. The full backend suite is green again (`BACKEND_EXIT=0`), and both artifacts are preserved OUTSIDE the gates for the next attempt: the working fix and the RED test, under `hg370-h19-*`.

WHAT THE NEXT ATTEMPT MUST DO DIFFERENTLY, stated so this is a specification rather than a lesson: the allocation fix has to **preserve the number and order of `selectExercises` calls** — call every family as before and vary only the COUNT requested and the final composition — or the novelty-budget consumption changes and pinned selection tests move. If the zero-quota families must still be called (with a count of zero, or with their floor share), the budget sequence is preserved and the criterion can be met without relocating a requirement.
ROUND 145 - I corrected my own record: the cause I stated for the H19 revert does not survive the arithmetic.

Last round I reverted a working H19 fix because it moved a pinned test, and I wrote the cause down as a fact: the shared novelty budget (`workoutBuilderService.mjs:583`) is created once and passed to every `selectExercises` call, so skipping zero-quota families changed budget consumption and therefore selection.

Checking that claim before building on it, the arithmetic refutes it:

  * the failing test calls `generate({ exerciseCount: 4, nasmPhase: 5 })`, and that helper defaults `category: 'full_body'` — six families;
  * the ORIGINAL asked `ceil(4/6) = 1` per family, in order, then sliced to the first four;
  * MY FIX gave quotas `1, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0` — the same counts for the same first four families, in the same order, so the budget state at each of those four calls was identical.

And a second fact makes the problem deeper than the explanation: the fixture's `ankle_hops` is declared `category: 'lunge'` — the FIFTH of the six families — so it should not have survived the original `slice(0, 4)` either. **My model of how the original selection produced `ankle_hops` is simply wrong**, not just my account of where the two allocators diverged. The honest position is that the ripple is MEASURED (the suite went red) but NOT EXPLAINED.

That distinction matters more than it looks. Had I left the confident cause in the record, the next attempt would have spent its budget testing a hypothesis that arithmetic had already killed, and might have "fixed" the allocation in a way that preserved call counts while the real coupling sat somewhere else entirely.

WHAT CHANGED IN THE RECORD: the H19 disposition row now states the cause as NOT ESTABLISHED, shows the refuting arithmetic, and names the diagnostic the next slice must run FIRST — log the per-family picks under both allocators for this exact fixture and find how a fifth-family exercise reached a four-item result before touching either allocator.

WHAT DID NOT CHANGE: the tree. No code was modified this round. The service is still byte-identical to HEAD, the RED test is still out of the suite, the backend suite is green (`hg371`), and `FRESHNESS_OK` holds on the recorded bytes. The working fix and the RED test remain preserved outside the gates as `hg370-h19-*` for the next attempt to start from.
ROUND 146 - H19's ripple is explained, and it was MY fix's fault, not the code's.

Round 144 reverted a working H19 fix because it moved a pinned test; round 145 refuted my own stated cause (the novelty budget) and left the mechanism unknown; this round found it, and the explanation predicts the observed output exactly.

THE FIXTURE IS SPARSE, and that is the whole story. `__tests__/workoutBuilderService.intelligence.test.mjs:31-36` defines a registry of **four exercises across three families** — squat (`goblet_squat`), hinge (`romanian_deadlift`), and lunge twice (`ankle_hops`, `walking_lunge`) — while a `full_body` day requests **SIX** families: push, pull, squat, hinge, lunge, core. **Three of the six requested families have nothing to return.**

  * ORIGINAL allocator, count 4: `ceil(4/6) = 1` for every family, called in order → push `[]`, pull `[]`, squat `[goblet_squat]`, hinge `[romanian_deadlift]`, lunge `[ankle_hops, …]`, core `[]` → three items, and `slice(0, 4)` truncates NOTHING because the list never reached four. That is how a FIFTH-family exercise survived a four-item request — my earlier confusion.
  * MY FIX: quotas `1, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0`, so the remainder went to the first four families BY ORDER — three of which can return nothing — and my `if (categoryQuota <= 0) continue;` **skipped lunge, the only remaining family with content**. Result: `[goblet_squat, romanian_deadlift]`. **Character for character the observed failure.**

So the cause is my own guard, not the shared novelty budget. The correction matters because it changes the fix: the problem is not budget consumption but the assumption that every family can fill its quota, which a sparse registry (routine at cold start) violates.

FIX DESIGN, ready for the next slice: **never skip a family** — call every one so order and budget sequence are untouched, and let a family with nothing return nothing — then, because a sparse registry legitimately leaves quota unfilled, **top up from families that still have content** rather than shipping a workout short of the requested count. That satisfies the criterion (no family dropped for want of slots) without relocating a pinned requirement.

The earlier novelty-budget explanation is WITHDRAWN in the record. No code changed this round: the service is still byte-identical to HEAD, the RED test is out of the suite, the backend suite is green and `FRESHNESS_OK` holds. The working fix and the RED test remain preserved outside the gates as `hg370-h19-*`.
ROUND 147 - A FALSE "LOST RECEIPT", caused by my own gate's hidden assumption about the working directory.

Closing this round I ran the freshness gate and got `PREFLIGHT_FAILED: no receipt at ...tmp\worktrees\.mega-blueprints\artifacts\b214f060bbec9038\READINESS-RECEIPT-20260913.md`. A missing authoritative receipt would have been the most serious possible finding, so I chased it before reporting anything - and it was not missing. `preflight-freshness.mjs` computed its root as `path.resolve(process.cwd(), '..')`: it silently ASSUMED the caller stood in `backend/`, as I had been doing all session. Run from the packet root, it built the wrong root, found no receipt there, and said so in words that name a lost packet rather than a wrong directory.

Two things make this a real defect and not just my slip. First, **the receipt itself documents the wrong invocation**: its freshness row is the one row in that table that gives a command WITHOUT the "(from `backend/`)" qualifier its siblings (drift audit, encoding scan) all carry. A reader following this packet's own instructions, from the directory this packet's own paths are written against, would have got a failure that accuses the packet of losing its receipt. Second, every content-hash comparison in that script is meaningless against a wrong root, so the failure mode is not a harmless mis-message - it is a verdict about the wrong tree, dressed as a verdict about this one.

Repair: resolve the root by walking up from CWD to a directory holding BOTH `backend/` and the receipt, and fail with `no packet root at or above <cwd>` plus the two things it looked for. Verified three ways - packet root `FRESHNESS_OK`, `backend/` `FRESHNESS_OK` (verdict unchanged, so no existing evidence is invalidated or superseded), unrelated directory the new loud diagnostic, exit 1. The receipt's freshness row now records that the gate is CWD-independent, so the documented command is true as written; TRAP 3 in the evidence index carries the lesson.

No product code was touched this round, so the freshness verdict, the manifest, and every area gate stand exactly as recorded. ROUND 148 - The sibling sweep found the same hidden assumption in two more gates, and THERE it was a FALSE PASS I had already read and nearly reported.

Rule 20 says a defect class is swept, not just the instance in front of you. I swept: `encoding-scan.mjs:33` and `drift-audit.mjs:43-44` carried the identical `path.resolve(process.cwd(), '..')`. On the freshness gate the wrong root produced a false FAILURE, which is loud and self-correcting. On these two it produces a false SUCCESS, which is neither: from the artifact directory `encoding-scan.mjs` enumerated ZERO files and printed `ENCODING_SCAN_OK`, exit 0, because the verdict is `invalid.length === 0` - vacuously true when nothing was read. From the packet root `drift-audit.mjs` crashed outright.

This is the uncomfortable part and it belongs in the record: **one command earlier in this same round I reported `ENCODING_SCAN_OK` from that exact wrong directory.** It was in my transcript as a green line. What caught it was not diligence about encoding - it was the freshness trap from the previous call making me ask which directory a verdict had been produced in. Had the freshness gate not failed first, a false pass would have gone into this round's report as evidence, in a packet whose entire purpose is that no claim outruns its evidence. That is how close the class came to working.

Repaired with the same walk-up plus a VACUITY GUARD in each, because the root bug and the vacuity bug are the same bug wearing two coats: `ok = <every count is zero>` is satisfied by having counted nothing. `encoding-scan` now fails on zero paths or zero text files; `drift-audit` fails on zero changed modules. A verdict whose every clause is a zero-count test is not a pass, it is an absence of evidence.

Verified as a matrix rather than a single run: three directories inside the packet (backend/, packet root, artifact dir) all now return IDENTICAL real counts - 435 changed/untracked paths, 85 changed backend modules, which agrees exactly with the rule-42 exposure of 63 untracked + 22 modified - and an unrelated directory makes all three refuse with exit 1. That was THIS test failing on the count=8 case, not the intelligence test. `ceil(8/6)=2` selected 12 across six families, `slice(0, 8)` kept the first four families, and lunge and core vanished - the contract's defect, reproduced exactly. Second, the fix needed TWO mechanisms, and the first attempt had only one. Clause 1 is now: floor+remainder allocation (so the trim can no longer be what deletes a family), a TOP-UP pass, and same-day dedupe. My first implementation had floor+remainder and the top-up but no dedupe, and it still broke a pinned test - `expected [ 'goblet_squat', …(3) ] to include 'ankle_hops'`. Filtering picks against what the day already holds fixed it. A day repeating an exercise is wrong on its own terms anyway. The top-up is equally load-bearing in the other direction: it is what gives a zero-quota family a second turn, so my round-146 diagnosis was right that the skip alone was not the bug - the skip with NO second chance was.

That is the same defect class as TRAP 3 in the evidence index, one layer over: a gate that reports the wrong cause sends the reader to fix the wrong thing. Repairing it produced a second, sharper mistake. The gate died with `ReferenceError: backend is not defined` - loud, which is the only mercy. Restored as `path.join(root, 'backend')`, which is what `process.cwd()` meant when the script was invoked from `backend/` and is now simply correct from anywhere. My first attempt at that restore also replaced the root-resolution block instead of appending to it; caught by re-reading the file rather than by trusting the edit.

Verified as a matrix, not a single run: `BOOT_GATE_OK` / `ROUTE_GRAPH_LINKS_OK` / exit 0 from the packet root AND from `backend/`, and `BOOT_GATE_FAILED: no packet root at or above <cwd>` with exit 1 from an unrelated directory (`hg383`). All five packet gates - boot, drift, encoding, freshness, markdown - are now CWD-independent and refuse loudly outside the packet, and freshness is unaffected: the boot log carries `BOOT_EXIT`, which is not the `BACKEND_EXIT` token the freshness detector selects on, so `hg376` remains the newest backend gate.

Three of the four false verdicts in this round came from HOW a gate was invoked or matched rather than what it measured: a wrong directory, a vacuous zero-count, and a phrase match. This one adds the sweep's own failure mode - checking the fixed set instead of the unfixed population.
ROUND 150 - H19 clause 3: a valid RED, a fix that half-worked, and a pinned expectation that says NO. Reverted, with the conflict recorded instead of papered over.

The defect is real and the criterion is right. `recentExerciseKeys` was ONE flat array that each generated day appended its ~6 keys to, and both consumers took `slice(-7)` of it — the last seven EXERCISES, roughly one session, while the comment on the array's own declaration, the contract, and the rotation-fallback arithmetic all assume seven SESSIONS. It is silent because the window is a SORT KEY, not a filter: `selectExercises:376-382` demotes recently-used work rather than excluding it, so a truncated window does not error or short a day, it merely stops demoting — an exercise from two to seven sessions ago comes back ahead of fresh alternatives.

VALID RED, and it needed the same rigour clause 1 did. My first version failed for a reason that was not the defect: the guard test I wrote to protect the assertion caught that the committed fixture's `core` pool is FOUR, where repeats inside seven sessions are arithmetically unavoidable and the service deliberately falls back (`eligiblePoolSize < 7`). Without that exclusion the test would have indicted the window for behaviour no correct implementation can avoid. Scoped to the categories that can actually rotate, the RED was 54 repeats inside a seven-session window, **every one of them `push`, every one at exactly four sessions apart** — the weekly push day re-picking the same exercises for the whole horizon despite an eight-exercise pool. That attribution is the part worth having: 54 violations in one category at one spacing is a mechanism, not noise.

THE FIX HALF-WORKED, AND THAT IS THE FINDING. A per-session window (last seven DAYS flattened, ~42 keys) took violations from 54 to 27 — real progress, and not enough — while `rotationFallback` appeared on 44 exercises and the pinned R6 test "with full equipment, no exercises are tagged rotationFallback" failed with `expected 44 to be +0`. The wider window SURFACES fallback conditions the narrow window was hiding. So clause 3's fix and R6's pinned expectation cannot both hold as written, and choosing between them decides what `rotationFallback` should MEAN — is a day a fallback because its pool is too small, or because a repeat actually occurred within the window? That is a product judgement about what the flag tells a trainer, not a mechanical edit, and it is exactly the kind of decision this packet's register reserves rather than assumes.

So I reverted, and verified the revert rather than asserting it: the service difference from HEAD is back to exactly the clause-1-only shape (`50/8`), `recentWindowKeys`/`RECENT_SESSION_WINDOW`/`recentSessionKeys` have zero occurrences left, `recentExerciseKeys` has its original four, clause 1 stays 4/4, the long-horizon suite returns to 9/9, and the full backend suite on the final bytes is 10324 passed / 1243 files / `BACKEND_EXIT=0` (`hg385`), byte-identical counts to the run before clause 3 was attempted. The freshness manifest corroborates the revert independently: its content hashes MATCHED, and the only failure was mtime ordering ("no backend gate log postdates the newest source file"), which is what a byte-exact restore looks like when the file has just been rewritten.

Two mechanics worth keeping. The revert's first attempt aborted because my anchor used LF while the file is CRLF — the trap already in this packet's own history — and it aborted LOUDLY because I asserted every replacement matched before writing, so nothing was half-applied; a second attempt failed on PowerShell parsing (`-join` binding wrongly against an inline comma inside a nested array literal) and was fixed by pre-assigning each string to a variable. Both failures were in my tooling, neither touched the service, and the file was never left partially reverted.

The RED test is preserved outside the suite as `hg384-h19-clause3-red-reverted-keeping-this.test.mjs` (154 lines), the disposition row now says clause 3 is RED-proven and blocked rather than merely unfixed, and the receipt's freshness row records the second re-recording (`hg386` record to `hg387` verify, margins 21:09:06 source vs 21:09:53 gate, 47 s). Clause 2 remains unsettled. Nothing about clause 3 is landed; nothing about clause 1 was disturbed.
ROUND 151 - H19 clause 3 IS LANDED. Last round I reverted it on a reading that one measurement overturned.

Last round I wrote that the session window's 44 `rotationFallback` tags CONFLICTED with the pinned R6 expectation, and reverted rather than choose. That framing was wrong, and the fix was to stop arguing about the tags and go and attribute them. A temporary probe (deleted after use, never part of the suite) reported the tags by category on a four-week plan: **push 0, pull 0, legs 0, core 12** - four on each of weeks 2, 3 and 4. Every tag sat on the single category whose pool is FOUR, smaller than the seven-session window, which is precisely where `eligiblePoolSize < 7` says fallback is expected; on the twelve-week plan R6 uses, 11 tagged core days x 4 = the 44 it saw, to the digit. The tags were HONEST REPORTING of an arithmetically forced repeat. The pinned expectations were the thing resting on the truncated window, because a window too short to notice `core` repeating can never fire the tag.

So the fix landed and the TESTS were corrected, each carrying its evidence. R6 now asserts zero tags on pools of 7 or more and exactly 44 on the thin pool, which also makes it discriminate the window. `workoutBuilderL1RoundFour` - the SECOND pinned test carrying the same over-broad global assertion, found only when the full suite went red - now scopes its legs-day assertion to the leg families its own title names, plus the invariant that no tag ever lands on a pool that could have honoured the window.

MY OWN CLAUSE-3 TEST WAS ALSO WRONG, for a reason worth keeping. It demanded ZERO repeats inside seven sessions. That is unreachable for this fixture by any implementation: a 6-exercise day whose category recurs every 4 sessions needs 12 distinct keys to cover two occurrences, while the largest rotatable pool holds 11 and push/pull hold 8. The first version therefore asserted what the inputs forbid - the same class of mistake as blaming the window for the thin core pool. It now pins the achievable bound: 54 violations under the seven-KEY window, 27 under seven SESSIONS, asserted as at most 30, a band that fails on regression. The bound is not a concession, it is the arithmetic, and the comment says so.

PROBE M58 is the discrimination for the whole slice: restoring `recentSessionKeys.flat().slice(-7)` fails BOTH dependent assertions - `expected +0 to be 44` (the core tags vanish) and `expected 54 to be less than or equal to 30` (the violations return to the broken value) - with the file restored SHA-256-identical and 11/11 green after. Two independent tests, two independent failure modes, one mutation.

FINAL STATE ON THE CHANGED BYTES: three H19 files 15/15 (clause 1 four tests, clause 3 two, long-horizon nine with the corrected R6). Full backend **10326 passed, 6 skipped, 1244 files, BACKEND_EXIT=0** (`hg394`) - and the delta closes exactly, +2 tests and +1 file over `hg385`, which is the restored two-test clause-3 file and nothing else. Gates: boot `BOOT_EXIT=0` (`hg395`), drift 90 modules / 19 reverse / 0 unresolved / 0 unexported / 0 unreadable (`hg396`), encoding 442 paths / 435 text / 7 binary / 0 invalid (`hg397`), freshness re-recorded on these bytes (`hg398` record, `hg399` verify, margins 21:13:46 source vs 21:14:22 gate, 36 s), markdown tables clean.

ROUND 152 - H19 IS COMPLETE. Clause 2 landed, and with it the discovery that my own clause-1 harness was measuring a fixture that was never used.

CLAUSE 2 (stored pattern beats category) is real in both directions, and I proved both before touching anything. `selectExercises` matched a family on the `category` LABEL alone (`:321-322`) and never consulted the stored `movementPattern`, even though the service reads that field for output mapping at `:239`, `:439` and `:759`. Two synthetic exercises made the defect concrete: one labelled `hinge` but stored as a SQUAT pattern was invisible to the squat family, and one labelled `squat` but stored as a HINGE pattern was handed to the squat family anyway. On a `legs` day (which resolves through `CATEGORY_MOVEMENT_MAP` to the squat family) the unfixed service returned `[''filler_squat'']` for the first and `[''filler_squat'',''pattern_hinge'']` for the second - a valid RED, two failures with two distinct causes.

THE LITERAL FIX WAS WRONG AND MEASUREMENT SAID SO. `ex.movementPattern || ex.category` turned clause 2 green, and broke the pinned NASM-5 test: `expected [ ''goblet_squat'', …(2) ] to include ''ankle_hops''`. The reason is worth keeping: `ankle_hops` stores `movementPattern: ''legs''`, and `legs` names no single FAMILY - it is a schedule alias expanding to squat + hinge + lunge (`:311`) - so letting it outrank the label makes the exercise unreachable from EVERY family slot in a full_body day. That is a capability regression, not a contract fix. The landed rule trusts the pattern only inside the family vocabulary (`push`, `pull`, `squat`, `hinge`, `lunge`, `core`) and otherwise falls back to the label: the stored movement wins when it names a movement, and a filing alias cannot silently delete an exercise. All five affected files then passed 23/23 together - clause 2 both directions, the NASM-5 opt-in, clause 1, clause 3 and the long-horizon rotation.

HARNESS DEFECT, FOUND BY ACCIDENT AND WORTH THE WHOLE ROUND. While building clause 2 I discovered that `generateWorkout:547` reads `getExerciseRegistryFromDB()` DIRECTLY and does NOT accept `registryOverride` - only `generatePlan:1219` does - and that both my test files mocked `exerciseRegistryService.mjs`, a module NOTHING imports; the service imports `getExerciseRegistryFromDB` from `variationEngine.mjs` (`:24-30`). My clause-2 fixture was therefore inert and the assertions were running against the service''s real fallback library: the first run returned `[''leg_extension'', ''wall_sit'']`, which are not my keys at all. That is a SETUP error wearing an assertion''s clothes, and it is exactly the failure this packet refuses as RED proof. Rewired to stub `variationEngine.mjs`, clause 2 produced a genuine RED immediately. **The lesson generalises beyond this file: a mock at the wrong specifier does not fail, it silently does nothing, and the test then measures whatever the real code would have done anyway.** The same defect sat in my clause-1 file, whose fixture was inert from the start and whose header claimed otherwise; its assertions were always measured against the real library, so its RED/GREEN and probe M57 stand as valid observations of real behaviour, but the claim is now corrected and the inert option removed from the file.

Probe M59 restores label-only membership and fails both clause-2 tests, with the file restored SHA-256-identical. Final state: full backend **10328 passed, 6 skipped, 1245 files, BACKEND_EXIT=0** (`hg401`) - +2 tests and +1 file over `hg394`, the new two-test clause-2 file and nothing else. Gates: boot `BOOT_EXIT=0` (`hg402`), drift 91 modules / 19 reverse / 0 unresolved / 0 unexported / 0 unreadable (`hg403`), encoding 443 paths / 436 text / 7 binary / 0 invalid (`hg404`), freshness re-recorded (`hg405` record, `hg406` verify, margins 21:20:01 source vs 21:20:41 gate, 40 s), markdown tables clean, zero stray probes.

H19 now stands with all three clauses built and verified, and the register row reads MET. Two follow-ups are recorded rather than hidden: the inert `const registry = FAMILIES.flatMap(...)` fixture block is still present in the clause-1 file (dead, now that the header explains why it was never used), and the clause-1/clause-2 harness correction means the rule "mock the module the service actually imports" deserves a sweep across the other workout-builder test files that pass `registryOverride`.
ROUND 153 - The inert-fixture sweep: five files, one phantom module, and a self-inflicted corruption I caught and repaired.

The sweep last round promised, done properly this time by grepping the POPULATION rather than the files I had already fixed. Two facts settled it. **`exerciseRegistryService.mjs` DOES NOT EXIST** - a directory scan of `services/` finds no such file - so every `vi.mock` of it is a no-op that creates a phantom module nothing imports. FIVE test files carry that mock: `workoutBuilderLongHorizon`, `workoutBuilderL1RoundFour`, and my three H19 files. The distinction that matters is which of them lose anything by it: the `generatePlan`-based files pass `registryOverride`, which `generatePlan:1219` honours directly, so their fixtures ARE the registry under test and the phantom mock is merely redundant; only `workoutBuilderMovementFamilies` drives `generateWorkout:547`, which ignores the override, and there the mock was part of a fixture that never ran.

Cleaned accordingly. My clause-1 file lost the inert fixture block and the phantom mock; my clause-3 file lost the phantom mock. All three H19 files stayed 8/8 green afterwards, which is the proof the removals were behaviour-preserving rather than a hope. The two pre-existing files had their misleading comment ("Stub the registry DB call") corrected in place, with the real reason recorded: the mock is a no-op and the override path is what actually works.

THEN I BROKE TWO FILES MYSELF, AND THE CAUSE IS IN THIS PACKET'S OWN NOTES. To place those comments I used a line-indexed edit built on `$text.Split($eol)`. PowerShell binds a one-string `.Split()` to the CHAR-ARRAY overload, so it split on CR and LF separately; rejoining with CRLF re-inserted a blank line at every break. `workoutBuilderLongHorizon` went 341 -> 717 lines and `workoutBuilderL1RoundFour` 240 -> 490, and in the latter my insert duplicated the `vi.mock(` opener, leaving an unclosed brace and a hard syntax error. **This is the second time this session the `.Split('literal')` trap has caught me, and it was already written in my own notes** - which is the argument for mechanical guards over memory: a note only helps if it is read at the moment of typing, and a helper that takes an EOL variable still walks into it. Repaired exactly: the duplicate opener removed, the doubled breaks collapsed in one pass (717 -> 360, 489 -> 246), both files syntax-clean, 13/13 green, and the line counts land where they should for the R6 correction (+19) and the legs scoping (+25), which is how I know the repairs preserved the intended content instead of merely producing valid JavaScript.

I also caught myself FABRICATING the freshness margins - I typed 21:26:11 and 21:26:52 into the receipt before reading what the tool had printed, and the real values were 21:22:30 and 21:23:10. Corrected to the tool's output. A margin is exactly the sort of small number that feels safe to estimate and is trivially checkable, so estimating it is indefensible.

FINAL STATE ON THE CHANGED BYTES: full backend **10328 passed, 6 skipped, 1245 files, BACKEND_EXIT=0** (`hg407`) - identical to `hg401`, so the sweep changed no behaviour. Gates: boot `BOOT_EXIT=0` (`hg408`), drift 91 modules / 19 reverse / 0 unresolved / 0 unexported / 0 unreadable (`hg409`), encoding 443 paths / 436 text / 7 binary / 0 invalid (`hg410`), freshness re-recorded (`hg411` record, `hg412` verify, margins 21:22:30 source vs 21:23:10 gate, 40 s), markdown tables clean.

ROUND 154 - H26/H27 are no longer unnameable. The requirement text was found, the rows were checked, and the verdict is PARTIAL with five named gaps.

The blocker on these two rows was never the code - it was that nobody had located their requirement text. Two rounds of notes said so ("unresolved pending the underlying audit text", "this packet makes no provider calls"), and the register carried H26 as NAMESPACE AMBIGUOUS and H27 as NEVER MENTIONED. Searching the blueprint docs for the band name and for a distinctive requirement phrase found both:

  * `docs/ai-workflow/blueprints/agent-ready-workout-planner-2026-09-06/13-server-repair-contract.md:278` = **R-H26** (validate dayType/mode at the Brain seam AND the Sprint create/update seam with shared enums; wire carries opaque tokens plus a canonical movement-pattern ID or unknown, a recent boolean, a setup bucket and a bounded headcount; no raw names, keys, notes, IDs, per-person selections or history text).
  * `:279` = **R-H27** (provider callback may accept a second options argument with AbortSignal and output/request bounds; one attempt; the existing 1-30 s deadline; abort on timeout, discard late success and rejection, clear handles; at most one unsettled optional Brain provider operation per server process, retaining occupancy when an adapter cannot cancel, with deterministic fallback for concurrent requests).
  * `:301` = the acceptance line, which names the test to extend and the six clauses to observe.
  * `15-audit-findings-and-fix-register.md:67-68` bands them "P1/P2 before provider activation" against backend boundaries B1-B2 and B3.

So the namespace doubt dissolves: the receipt's `S-H26/27, S-H30` row and this register's H26/H27 are the same requirement, seen from the contract's side.

THE CHECK, against `services/bootcamp/bootcampBrain.mjs` and `tests/unit/bootcampBrain.test.mjs`. **Seven clauses are already met** and I cite each: the 1-30 s clamp (`:165-166`, with a test pinning the 1 s floor), timeout as a named `brain_timeout` that still generates the class (`test:119`), the timer cleared (`:207`), opaque tokens only with no raw key on the wire (`:95-109`, tests `:140` and `:148`), an oversized reply dropped before parse (`:154`), a hallucinated key rejecting the whole ordering (`:82`, `:114`), and default-off flag behaviour (`:98`).

**Five gaps, and the two behavioural ones are real.** R-H27's AbortSignal does not exist: the timeout is a bare `Promise.race` (`:170-172`) and the provider callback is never handed a second options argument or a signal, so an adapter is RACED rather than ABORTED - the requirement says "where supported", and nothing asks whether it is supported. Occupancy containment does not exist at all: nothing tracks "at most one unsettled optional Brain provider operation per server process", nothing retains occupancy for an adapter that cannot cancel, and there is no deterministic concurrent fallback. R-H27 states the trap explicitly - "do not release the occupancy merely because Promise.race returned" - which is precisely the bug a race-based implementation invites, and this one has no occupancy to release. The three unproven clauses are narrower: "disabled zero calls" is asserted only as an OUTCOME (`:98` checks the heuristic result and a null fallback reason but never spies the callback, so zero-call behaviour is untested), late-result discard is implicit in the race with no test, and handle clearing is exercised only indirectly.

Separately, R-H26's enum validation is absent at the Brain seam - `dayTypeId` is interpolated straight into the prompt at `:98` with no shared-enum check - and the Sprint create/update seam has not been located, so that half is NOT CHECKED rather than failed. ROUND 155 - R-H27 LANDED: AbortSignal and per-process occupancy containment, with the containment immediately exposing two tests that had been lying about their providers.

The two behavioural gaps this round's check identified are now closed in `services/bootcamp/bootcampBrain.mjs`. The provider callback receives a SECOND options argument - `{ signal, maxOutputChars: 64_000, attempt: 1 }` - so the abort channel and the request/output bounds travel with the call, and `controller.abort()` fires when the deadline expires. The callback was previously handed the prompt alone and merely RACED against a timer, which is not the same thing: an adapter that supports cancellation was never asked to cancel.

OCCUPANCY IS THE HALF WORTH READING. A process-level counter admits at most one unsettled optional Brain operation; a concurrent caller gets `brain_busy` and a deterministic heuristic pool WITHOUT reaching the adapter. The slot is released when the PROVIDER settles, not when the race returns - the requirement says so outright ("do not release the occupancy merely because Promise.race returned"), and it is the difference between containing a runaway adapter and pretending to. A `providerPromise.then(release, release)` does that and doubles as the handler that stops a late provider failure becoming an unhandled rejection.

THE CONTAINMENT IMMEDIATELY CAUGHT THE TEST SUITE CHEATING. Six existing tests went red with `brain_busy`, and the cause was not the implementation: two of them fed the brain `() => new Promise(() => {})`, a provider that NEVER settles. Under real containment that correctly holds the process's only slot forever, so every later test in the file was being answered by a slot the previous test had abandoned. They now use a provider that settles LATE, which exercises the same timeout path and returns the slot. This is worth recording as a find rather than a fix: the old tests were passing while describing a provider that could not exist in production, and the containment is what made the description load-bearing.

Then the new tests failed for a second reason of my own making, and it is the more embarrassing one: I wrote them against `run()` WITHOUT setting the deadline, so they inherited the **8000 ms default**, and a 1300 ms "late" settlement was early. They reported `provider_error` where I expected `timeout`, and one reported `llm` where I expected `heuristic` - the reply genuinely WAS on time. Setting `SWAN_BOOTCAMP_BRAIN_TIMEOUT_MS: ''50''` fixed it, which also means every one of these tests runs against the 1000 ms CLAMP FLOOR rather than the number written in the env, a subtlety I had to rediscover when a 250 ms settlement beat a 50 ms deadline that was never really 50 ms.

FIVE TESTS ADDED to `tests/unit/bootcampBrain.test.mjs` (now 291 lines, 27 passing): the signal arrives unaborted on the second argument with its bounds; the deadline aborts it; a late SUCCESS is discarded and the heuristic stands; a concurrent operation is contained and the second callback is never invoked; and with the flag off the provider is never called at all. **Probe M60** deletes `controller.abort()` and the occupancy guard together and fails exactly those two - `expected false to be true` and `expected 1 to be +0`, two independent mutations with two independent failure modes - then restores SHA-256-identically and returns to 27/27.

I went looking for the shared enums R-H26 requires and found the seam, the registry, and a defect that the code had already named for a different field. `buildBrainPrompt` (`services/bootcamp/bootcampBrain.mjs:101-118`) builds the provider prompt from two RAW interpolations: `dayTypeId` at `:104` ("for a ${dayTypeId} group class") and `headcount` at `:105` ("of ${headcount} people"). R-H26 asks for precisely this seam's validation - "validate dayType/mode ... using shared enums", "canonical movement-pattern ID or unknown", "bounded headcount", "no raw names/keys/notes" - so the requirement and the defect coincide, and the shared enum already exists: `dayTypeContract.mjs:142` exports `getDayTypeRegistry()`, whose contract documents `lower_body | upper_body | cardio | full_body` (`:157`), and the Brain seam has exactly ONE caller (`bootcampGenerator.mjs:645`, passing `dayTypeId: dayType`, `mode: equipmentProfileId ? ''strict'' : ''open_gym''`, `headcount: expectedParticipants`), so the domain is knowable rather than guessed.

What IS verified and recorded: the seam has one caller; the registry is the shared enum source and already accepts the id the existing tests use; the mode enum is closed at two values; and both raw interpolations are real, quoted above. The fix uses the shared enum, not a local one. `buildBrainPrompt` now validates `dayTypeId` against `getDayTypeRegistry()` from `dayTypeContract.mjs:142` - the same registry the day-type contract itself applies - and sends the literal `unknown` when the id is unrecognised, which is the register's own wording ("canonical movement-pattern ID or unknown"). I did NOT implement it, and the reason is the same one that has held all session: it changes what the model SEES, so it needs its own RED tests, a probe, the full suite and a freshness re-record, and this round's budget did not cover that. TWO DEFECTS SURFACED WHILE DOING IT, BOTH MINE.

The first is a false fact I injected in round 157 and only saw because I printed the prompt instead of reasoning about it. `Number(null)` is 0, NOT NaN, so my headcount clamp turned an ABSENT headcount into "of 1 people" - the prompt stating a fact nobody supplied, in a function whose entire job this round was to stop unsupplied text reaching the model. Absence is now preserved explicitly. I had "verified" that clamp with tests that passed a real number and one that passed a string of text; neither covered the case of passing nothing, which is the case that actually occurs by default.

The second is my own assertion going stale against my own change. The cross-seam test asserted `not.toContain('unknown')` to prove the day type was not being stamped unknown - sound while `unknown` could come from nowhere else, and wrong the moment the register's own `pattern=unknown` became a required fact. The full suite caught it, not the focused run, which is a useful reminder that the focused run proves what I was thinking about and the suite proves what I was not.

AND ONE MECHANICAL FAILURE WORTH RECORDING: my four-pair edit reported all four pairs applied and wrote a file in which two of them were absent - twice, in successive calls. I caught it only by printing the resulting prompt and noticing a required line was missing, then by reading the function. The lesson I have now learned three times over is to verify an edit by its EFFECT (print the artefact) and not by the loop's own report.

ROUND 171 - THE HOSTILE REVIEW FOUND A REAL DEFECT, AND IT IS MINE TWICE OVER. R-H27's containment can wedge permanently, and my own suite caught it before the reviewer did.

VERDICT: DEFECT FOUND, reproduced, NOT FIXED. `unsettledOptionalBrainOps` is released ONLY when the provider promise settles. The reviewer executed five calls against a provider that never settles: #1 `timeout` (the abort DID fire, `signal.aborted=true`), #2 and #3 `brain_busy`, then #4 and #5 with a HEALTHY provider returning a valid reply - and both still returned `brain_busy` with the adapter invoked ZERO further times. One adapter invocation, then the optional Brain is dead for the process lifetime. Nothing resets the counter, so even toggling `SWAN_BOOTCAMP_BRAIN` off and back on in-process does not clear it; only a restart does.

REACHABILITY IS NOT HYPOTHETICAL, and the evidence is uncomfortable because it comes from my own code and comments. Production resolves `completionFn: await resolveBrainProvider()` (`bootcampGenerator.mjs:651`), and the provider contract is documented as "an ES module whose default export is `async (prompt) => string`" (`:813-817`) - `signal` is NOT part of that contract. My own comment at `bootcampBrain.mjs:252-253` says "An adapter that ignores the signal is unharmed", which is exactly the admission that an adapter may keep running forever. So a stalled adapter is permitted by the contract, and the containment turns it into a permanent outage of the feature. That is precisely the "before provider activation" gate R-H27 exists to close, and the feature is currently opt-in, which is the only reason this is latent rather than live.

THE WORST PART IS THAT I ALREADY KNEW. In round 155 the 27-test brain suite went red with six `brain_busy` failures. I diagnosed the mechanism correctly, wrote it down in `bootcampBrain.test.mjs` in these words - a never-settling provider "would hold this process's only slot for the rest of the file and every later test would see `brain_busy`" - and then changed the two providers from `() => new Promise(() => {})` to a late-settling `lateProvider()`, and recorded the episode in the ledger as a TEST problem. The failure mode was observed, described accurately in my own handwriting, and worked around in the fixture instead of bounded in the code. That comment was a production hazard sitting in a test file, and I read it as housekeeping. The reviewer put it best: the suite caught it and my diff removed the detector.

SECONDARY LATENT HAZARD, also recorded: `new AbortController()` at `:236` sits between the increment (`:234`) and the release attachment (`:247`) and OUTSIDE the `try` (`:238`), so if it throws, the counter wedges with no provider promise in existence. Not reachable on Node 15+ since the global always exists, so it is an ordering defect rather than a demonstrated production path; the fix is to increment after `:236` or move it inside the `try`.

WOULD MY 12 SEAM TESTS CATCH IT? No. All twelve inject an immediately-settling provider; none asserts on the counter or on `brain_busy`; 39/39 pass with the wedge fully exploitable. The packet's probe `backend-boundary-probes.mjs:88-108` misses it too, because its provider settles at 1100ms.

FIX DIRECTION, RECORDED NOT APPLIED: bound the hold - release the slot on the abort path after an explicit grace period or TTL, with a single-release guard so a later settlement cannot decrement twice - and add the regression test the suite currently refuses to write, using a GENUINELY never-settling provider and asserting that a later call still reaches the adapter. I did not attempt it: this session has no budget left to implement, probe, re-run the full suite and re-record, and landing an unverified fix to a containment bug would repeat the mistake that created it.

PACKET STATE: the H26/H27 status cell no longer reads as a clean landing - it carries "BUT CARRIES A REPRODUCED HIGH DEFECT ... NOT FIXED", with the full evidence in the row. Freshness was also restored this round: the round-170 revert rewrote a frontend source file, and even though the content hashes still matched, the ordering clause requires a gate newer than the newest source, so the full frontend suite was re-run (8491 passed / 1644 files / `FRONTEND_EXIT=0`, `hg451`) and the receipt's frontend citation and record log were re-pointed (`hg452` record, then `FRESHNESS_OK`). Markdown tables clean.
ROUND 172 - THE WEDGE IS FIXED, WITH THE REGRESSION TEST THE SUITE HAD REFUSED TO WRITE.

The round-171 review was right and the fix is small. Three changes in `bootcampBrain.mjs`:

  1. `OCCUPANCY_GRACE_MS = 1_500` and a grace release on the abort path. The slot is still held until the provider settles - that IS the requirement - but "until settlement" can no longer be unbounded. After the deadline we abort, give the adapter a bounded grace period to honour that abort, then release the slot. The timer is `unref`ed so it cannot hold the event loop open.
  2. A SINGLE-RELEASE GUARD (`occupancyReleased`). Without it a grace release followed by a late settlement would decrement twice and let two providers run at once - a worse bug than the one being fixed.
  3. `new AbortController()` and the release closure now precede the increment. They used to sit between the increment and the release attachment, OUTSIDE the `try`, so a throw there wedged the counter with no provider promise in existence at all.

THE REGRESSION TEST EXISTS NOW. `bootcampBrainSeamInput.test.mjs` gains the test the 27-test suite had worked around: a GENUINELY never-settling provider (`new Promise(() => {})`), then a healthy one, asserting the healthy adapter is REACHED. It was RED first, and the RED is the best evidence in this round: `expected +0 to be 1` on the healthy-call count, with the retry reporting `brain_busy` on its FIRST call - the wedge visible twice in one run, including proof that it survives across attempts because nothing cleared the counter.

After the fix: both brain files **40/40**, full backend **10346 passed / 6 skipped / 1246 files / BACKEND_EXIT=0** (`hg454`) - exactly +1 over `hg441`, the new test and nothing else. Gates: boot `BOOT_EXIT=0` (`hg455`), drift `DRIFT_EXIT=0` (`hg456`), encoding `ENCODING_EXIT=0` (`hg457`), freshness re-recorded (`hg458` record, `hg459` verify), markdown clean.

The fix's real signal was in the OTHER assertion passing: the healthy adapter HAD been reached.

WHAT THIS ROUND ACTUALLY TEACHES, stated plainly because it is the most useful thing here: the defect existed for sixteen rounds and my own suite found it in round 155. I diagnosed it correctly, wrote the mechanism into a comment, and then edited the fixture so the suite went green. The comment I wrote - "would hold this process''s only slot for the rest of the file and every later test would see brain_busy" - was a production hazard sitting in a test file, and I read it as housekeeping. The hostile reviewer did not find something I had missed; it found something I had written down and then walked past. That is the argument for the review step existing at all, and for treating a test that goes red for a plausible reason as a finding rather than an obstacle.
ROUND 173 - Re-review of the FIX put in flight, because a fix landed after the review.

The objective's final phase is review until dry, and a fix that lands AFTER a review is exactly the thing that has not been reviewed. So the round-172 change went back out to a narrow, time-boxed reviewer with ONE question: can the grace release fire at a moment when the occupancy it releases belongs to a DIFFERENT operation than the one that armed it - i.e. can a stale grace timer free a slot a newly admitted operation is holding, letting two provider operations run at once? It was told to consider four specific paths: a stale grace timer surviving a late settlement; whether `unref()` can mean the release simply never happens in a real process lifecycle; whether two operations can both hold the counter at 1; and whether the bounded hold now RELEASES WHILE THE PROVIDER IS STILL RUNNING - which would contradict R-H27's own words, "do not release the occupancy merely because Promise.race returned" - in which case it was told to say so explicitly and name the tradeoff rather than treat it as acceptable. It was also asked whether the new test would catch whatever it finds.

That fourth path is the one I am least sure of myself, and it is worth stating rather than waiting to be told: the grace release DOES release while a non-settling provider may still be running, and that is a deliberate departure from a literal reading of the requirement. The alternative is the defect that was just fixed - a permanent wedge - so the honest position is that the requirement's intent (do not double-spend a single provider operation, and contain concurrency to one) is preserved while its literal mechanism (hold until settlement) is bounded. If the reviewer judges that tradeoff wrong, the fix needs a different shape rather than a tweak, and I would rather hear that from a reviewer than decide it myself.

NO CODE CHANGED THIS ROUND. The service sits at 121/7, both brain files at 40/40, full backend 10346 passed / 1246 files / BACKEND_EXIT=0 (`hg454`), all five packet gates green, `FRESHNESS_OK`. Nothing is claimed about the fix beyond what round 172 executed and recorded; the re-review verdict is outstanding.
ROUND 174 - Probe M64: the bounded hold verified end to end, independently of the regression test.

While the re-review runs, I executed the exact race I had asked it about rather than waiting to be told. `hg460-probe-M64-grace-release.mjs` (run from %TEMP%, no repo file touched, preserved in the artifacts directory) does three things against the fixed service:

  1. Operation A with a provider that NEVER settles, deadline clamped to 1000ms -> `timeout` (unchanged, as it should be).
  2. Wait past the deadline PLUS the grace period (2600ms > 1000 + 1500). Operation B with a HEALTHY provider -> **1 adapter call, `brainUsed: 'llm'`**. The wedge is gone: the adapter is reachable again after a hung call.
  3. Operation C after B completed -> **1 adapter call, `llm`**. The slot genuinely returns to zero after a normal operation, so the grace path does not leave the counter stuck in the other direction either.

Why this matters beyond the suite: the regression test asserts the SAME property, so on its own it is one artifact agreeing with itself. The probe is an independent, executed demonstration of the full sequence - hung call, grace expiry, healthy call admitted, second healthy call admitted - and it is the shape a production process would actually experience.

WHAT IT DOES NOT PROVE, stated so the coverage is not overread: it exercises the sequence where the hung operation's provider never settles at all. It does not construct the stale-timer variant where an operation's provider settles LATE, after its own grace release has already fired, and a NEW operation has since claimed the slot. My reading of the code is that the single-release guard (`occupancyReleased`) makes that path safe - the late settlement finds the guard set and returns without decrementing - but that reading is exactly the kind of reasoning this session has repeatedly shown to be insufficient, which is why it is one of the four paths I asked the re-reviewer to attack with a counterexample rather than accept from me. Until that verdict lands, M64 is evidence about the never-settling path ONLY.

No code changed this round. Service 121/7, both brain files 40/40, full backend 10346 passed / 1246 files / `BACKEND_EXIT=0` (`hg454`), all five packet gates green, `FRESHNESS_OK`.
ROUND 175 - Probe M65 closes the one path I was least sure of, and it closes it with an execution rather than a reading.

The gap I flagged last round was the stale-timer variant: an operation whose provider settles LATE, after its own grace release has fired, while a NEW operation holds the slot. If that late settlement decremented again, it would free the newcomer's slot and let two provider operations run at once - a worse bug than the wedge that was just fixed. I said my reading of the `occupancyReleased` guard made it safe, and that a reading was not good enough. So I built the sequence and ran it (`hg461-probe-M65-stale-timer.mjs`, run from %TEMP%, preserving in the artifacts directory):

  * t=1006ms - A times out against a 1000ms deadline although its provider succeeds at 3000ms, so the late SUCCESS is discarded as R-H27 requires.
  * t=2608ms - past A's grace (2500ms), B claims the slot with a provider that never settles.
  * t=3014ms - **A's late settlement arrives WHILE B holds the slot**, and a third operation C attempts. C returns `brain_busy` with ZERO adapter calls.
  * t=10613ms - B reaches its own 8000ms deadline and reports `timeout`.

So the stale release does NOT free B's slot: the per-operation guard blocks the late decrement, and the counter's meaning survives. The exact race I was unsure about is now decided by execution, not by my reading of it.

THE SAME RUN ALSO EXPOSES THE TRADEOFF MORE SHARPLY, and it belongs in the record rather than in a footnote. B's grace released its slot at roughly 4100ms, while B's own race did not return until 10613ms. So after the grace period the slot IS free even though the operation that held it has not finished - a third operation would have been admitted at, say, 5000ms. That is the deliberate departure from R-H27's literal "do not release the occupancy merely because Promise.race returned": the hold is bounded, not unbounded, and the bound can expire while an adapter is still running. The choice is between that and a permanent wedge, and I am recording it as a choice with its cost rather than presenting the fix as free. If the hostile re-review judges the tradeoff wrong, the fix needs a different shape - for example making the provider contract mandate `signal` handling and hard-failing adapters that ignore it - not a tweak to the grace period.

STATUS OF THE REVIEW: still outstanding. But three of the four paths I delegated are now answered by my own executions (M64 for the never-settling admission path, M65 for the stale timer, and the suite's `unref` and single-holder cases), so the open question has narrowed to the tradeoff itself rather than to correctness of the mechanism.

No code changed this round. Service 121/7, both brain files 40/40, full backend 10346 passed / 1246 files / `BACKEND_EXIT=0` (`hg454`), all five gates green, `FRESHNESS_OK`.

If that judgement goes against the fix, the fix needs a different SHAPE - making the provider contract mandate `signal` handling and hard-failing adapters that ignore it - not a longer grace period.

ROUND 179 - The freshness gate is STALE on an ORDERING technicality, diagnosed precisely and left for the next round rather than papered over.

WHAT HAPPENED. Fixing the S04 thumbnail touched `NASMExerciseRolodex.list.tsx` at 21:58:55 and `NASMExerciseRolodex.previewHighlightSync.test.tsx` in the same pass. The frontend gate log for that work (`hg462`) was written at 21:58:30 - twenty-five seconds EARLIER - because it was the run I did to LOCATE the failures, before the repair. So the newest frontend source is now newer than the newest frontend gate, and the freshness preflight refuses both to record and to pass: "no frontend gate log postdates the newest source file".

THE PROTOCOL ORDER WAS THE MISTAKE, and it is a repeat of a lesson this packet already contains. The order is GATES, then RECORD, then RECEIPT. I edited frontend sources AFTER running the frontend gate, which invalidates the ordering the gate exists to protect. The correct sequence for the thumbnail work was: apply the repair, run the frontend suite, then record, then cite. I ran the suite to diagnose, then repaired, and never re-ran it afterwards - so the gate is genuinely older than the bytes it would certify.

WHAT I TRIED, AND WHY IT DID NOT RESOLVE IT. I re-ran the full frontend suite afterwards (`hg471-frontend-full-after-thumb-repair.log`, 8491 passed / `FRONTEND_EXIT=0`, written after the repair) and re-pointed the receipt at it. The preflight still named `hg462` as the newest frontend gate and still refused to record, reporting `must include hg465, hg462` and `missing hg462`. That is consistent with the tool resolving the newest per-area gate from the last SUCCESSFUL manifest rather than scanning the directory afresh: because the record step had been refusing, the manifest still held `hg462`, so `hg471` was never considered. I have NOT confirmed that mechanism by reading the tool, and I am labelling it as the best explanation rather than as a fact - this packet has taught me what unverified mechanisms are worth.

STATE LEFT, deliberately honest rather than green. The receipt cites `hg465` for the backend (10347 passed / 1246 files) and `hg462` for the frontend, and its freshness row asserts a passing state that the preflight does not currently agree with. The single remaining complaint is the ordering one: the frontend gate log predates the frontend source by 25 seconds. **Everything else is verified on the current bytes** - full backend `hg465` and full frontend `hg471` both exit 0 (8491 passed), the explicit heap-gated type-check is exit 0 with zero errors, boot/drift/encoding are clean, and markdown tables are clean. No source file is in doubt; only the ORDERING EVIDENCE is.

NEXT ROUND, in this order and no other: run the frontend suite once more (it will be newer than every frontend source), then `--record`, then cite whatever gate the tool names in the receipt row, then verify `FRESHNESS_OK`. That is three mechanical steps. I am stopping here rather than starting them with no context left, because the failure mode of half-finishing this sequence is the one that produced the problem.
ROUND 180 - The freshness ordering failure is RESOLVED, and the packet is coherent again.

Three steps, in the order the protocol requires, and the order is the whole story. (1) Ran the frontend suite AFTER the last source edit: `hg474-frontend-full-fresh.log`, 8491 passed / 1644 files / `FRONTEND_EXIT=0`. (2) `--record` succeeded: `RECORDED 8695 file hashes from gate logs hg465-backend-full-guard-test.log / hg474-frontend-full-fresh.log`. (3) Cited `hg474` in the receipt's freshness row. Result: **FRESHNESS_OK** - every source file matches the recorded gate-time bytes, both areas have a gate newer than their newest source, and the receipt asserts a passing state. Markdown tables clean.

ONE THING I GOT WRONG LAST ROUND, corrected here. I had re-run the frontend suite earlier (`hg471`) and the preflight still named `hg462`, and I wrote down a hypothesis - that the tool resolves per-area gates from the last successful manifest rather than scanning afresh. Reading the tool's selection code showed that hypothesis was WRONG: it does `readdirSync` over the artifacts directory and qualifies a log by `FRONTEND_EXIT=` plus one of `RUN v\d|Test Files|PARSE_OK|ROUTE_GRAPH_LINKS_OK|changed/untracked backend`, so it scans afresh. I checked `hg471` afterwards and it carries ALL THREE markers, so it should have qualified. Why it was not picked I have NOT established, and the honest statement is that a subsequent fresh run (`hg474`) was picked immediately with identical markers and a newer mtime. The likeliest explanation is a timestamp or flush artefact on the run I did mid-diagnosis, but I am labelling that as unconfirmed rather than inventing a mechanism - which is exactly the discipline the round-179 note failed at, where I wrote a plausible mechanism into the record as if it were a finding.

WHAT THE ORDERING RULE ACTUALLY BUYS, restated because I violated it twice in two rounds: the manifest is a set of content hashes taken at gate time, and the ordering clause is what makes those hashes MEAN anything - a gate older than the bytes it certifies has verified something else. Editing sources after gating, which is what I did, does not corrupt the code; it corrupts the EVIDENCE, and evidence is this packet's entire product. The remedy is mechanical, which is why it is worth following literally instead of reasoning about.

Same failure shape rule 42 documents for the backend, reached through the frontend, in the very file the S04 repair created this session. The receipt now carries the importer name, its line, and its tracked/modified status rather than the weaker sentence it had.

A rule-42 audit restricted to `backend/` would have passed cleanly and the next commit would still have broken the build.

ROUND 191 - The generator review's interim report names a DEFECT CANDIDATE IN MY OWN FIX, and I am recording it before it is confirmed because the fix direction is already clear.

THE CLAIM (c), unexecuted at the time of writing. In the top-up loop I added at `workoutBuilderService.mjs:644`, the requested count is applied INSIDE `takeFrom(moveCat, 1)` and the already-chosen filter runs AFTER it. So each family can only ever offer its RANK-0 pick: if rank-0 for that family is already in `chosenKeys`, the filter empties the result, the loop `continue`s, and when every family does that the loop exits with `progressed = false` and the day is SHORT. The reviewer's concrete scenario: `full_body`, `exerciseCount` 8, knee-pain `excludedMuscles: ['quads']` emptying the lunge family while push/pull/squat/hinge/core hold five each - the new code returns 7 of 8, and HEAD returned 8 of 8.

WHY I BELIEVE IT IS REAL, from reading my own code rather than from the report. The mechanism is exactly what that loop does: `takeFrom(moveCat, 1)` cannot return rank-1 because the count bounds the selection before the filter sees it. And the HEAD comparison is credible for a reason worth stating plainly: the OLD code used `ceil` per family and sliced, so in that scenario it produced 10 candidates and returned 8 - four families represented, lunge dropped. Clause 1's whole purpose was to stop families vanishing, and if (c) holds then in sparse-pool cases my fix traded "8 exercises, one family missing" for "7 exercises, one family missing". That is a SHORT DAY, trainer-visible, and it would be a regression I introduced in round 149.

THE FIX DIRECTION, identified but NOT APPLIED. Pass the SHORTFALL as the count instead of 1 - `takeFrom(moveCat, exerciseCount - selectedExercises.length)` - then filter and slice. A family whose rank-0 is taken would then return rank-0 AND rank-1, the filter drops the taken one, and rank-1 fills the slot. That preserves the dedupe and the bound while making every family's deeper ranks reachable.

WHY I HAVE NOT EDITED THE FILE. The review is still RUNNING against the current bytes, and I told it so. Editing `workoutBuilderService.mjs` now would invalidate any reproduction it is mid-way through producing and would invalidate the exact artifact I most need - an EXECUTED counterexample rather than a hypothesis. The file stays untouched until the verdict arrives.

HYPOTHESIZED SAFE, with the reviewer's reasons, all still unexecuted: (a) the loop terminates because `takeFrom(cat, 1)` returns at most one item and every `progressed = true` iteration strictly grows `selectedExercises`, bounded by `exerciseCount`; (b) no duplicate within a day, because `familyOf()` is single-valued so the six full_body pools are disjoint and the quota pass cannot collide; (d) never more than requested, because `slice(0, exerciseCount)` bounds it. Two useful side confirmations also came back: no leftover `recentExerciseKeys` reference remains anywhere, and `recentlyUsedExercises` is only a SORT KEY (`filterExercises:184` builds a `recentSet` it never actually uses), so clause 3 cannot lengthen or shorten a day - which independently corroborates the round-146 diagnosis that the truncated window could only demote, never drop.

ROUND 192 - The round-191 claim is upgraded from hypothesis to MECHANICALLY CONFIRMED, and the register no longer reads as a clean MET.

I did not need to wait for the execution to settle the mechanism. Reading the exact line I wrote settles it: `workoutBuilderService.mjs:644` is

    const picked = takeFrom(moveCat, 1).filter((exercise) => exercise.key && !chosenKeys.has(exercise.key));

`takeFrom(moveCat, 1)` asks the family for exactly ONE exercise, so selection is bounded BEFORE the filter runs. If that one is already in `chosenKeys`, the filter empties the result and the loop `continue`s - and the next `while` iteration asks the SAME family again and receives THE SAME rank-0 pick, because selection is deterministic for the same inputs. **A family whose top pick is already chosen can therefore never contribute its deeper ranks at all.** When the remaining slots could only be filled by those deeper ranks, the day comes up short. That is certain from the line; only the MAGNITUDE needs the reviewer's execution, and its scenario (`full_body`, count 8, quads-excluded emptying lunge, other families holding five each) predicts 7 of 8 against HEAD's 8 of 8.

An important consequence I had to correct rather than note: **the H19 row claimed MET, and that was no longer true.** Clause 1 fixed families vanishing and introduced a short-day path in sparse-pool cases; a register that keeps saying MET while a reviewer is executing a counterexample against it is precisely the stale-claim failure this packet corrected four times. The row now reads PARTIAL, names the defect, quotes the offending line, states the one-line fix, and says why the fix is not applied yet - the review is still running against these bytes and a change now would invalidate the very reproduction I want.

THE FIX, READY AND DELIBERATELY UNAPPLIED: pass the shortfall as the count - `takeFrom(moveCat, exerciseCount - selectedExercises.length)` - then filter and slice. A family whose rank-0 is taken would then return rank-0 AND rank-1; the filter drops the taken one and keeps rank-1. It needs a RED test, the full backend suite and a freshness re-record before it can be called verified, and this session has no budget left for that sequence. Landing it unverified would be the same class of mistake as the defect itself: a change made faster than it could be checked.

WHAT THIS ROUND IS ACTUALLY WORTH: three reviews have now run this session and each found something real - the occupancy wedge, the bounded-grace deviation plus a blind test, and now a defect in the fix that closed the previous finding. Each was in code written by the same author, in the same session, and each was found by a NARROW question with a required counterexample rather than by a broad sweep. The generator review in particular asked one question with four named sub-parts and produced, in its interim report alone, a defect candidate in my own work that I had already read past several times.

ROUND 193 - THE SHORT-DAY DEFECT IS FIXED, RED captured on the pre-fix bytes.

The round-191/192 finding is closed. The top-up loop now asks for the SHORTFALL rather than 1: `const shortfall = exerciseCount - selectedExercises.length; const picked = takeFrom(moveCat, shortfall).filter(...).slice(0, shortfall);`. With a count of 1 the selection was bounded BEFORE the already-chosen filter ran, so a family whose rank-0 was taken could never offer rank-1, and the loop re-asked the same family for the same top pick indefinitely. The shortfall lets the filter skip a taken rank-0 and keep rank-1; the slice preserves the bound.

THE RED IS THE DISCRIMINATION and it ran on exactly the bytes the fix changed. `backend/__tests__/workoutBuilderTopUpReach.test.mjs` builds seven eligible exercises across six families - five families with one each, one EMPTY, one with three - and asks a full_body day for eight. Eight is impossible; SEVEN is the requirement. Before: `expected [ 'push_only', 'pull_only', …(3) ] to have a length of 7 but got 5`, with core_b and core_c stranded. After: 7 of 7.

ROUND 194 - The reviewer was told the code changed, because a verdict against stale bytes is not a verdict.

The generator review has been running across rounds 191-193, and round 193 landed a fix to the exact line it was reviewing. Left alone, its final verdict would have been written against bytes that no longer exist - and worse, it might have reported the defect I had already fixed, which would read as a new finding and waste the next reader's attention. So it was sent the change: the old line, the new line, the rationale, the new test and its RED.

It was asked for three things and only three: re-run ITS OWN scenario (full_body, exerciseCount 8, quads excluded) against the current bytes and report the EXECUTED number, whether 8 of 8 or still short - with the explicit note that either answer is fine because what I want is the number and not agreement; whether the shortfall-based count introduces any NEW problem I have missed, naming three specific worries (`.slice(0, shortfall)` truncating something it should keep, a large count changing `selectExercises` behaviour in a way that matters, and whether (a), (b) and (d) remain SAFE under the new line); and its verdict on the CURRENT code.

That is the "review until dry" discipline applied literally rather than ceremonially. A loop that never tells its reviewers what changed converges on a moving target, and this packet has already seen the cost of reviewing the wrong thing: the round-189 lesson was that my own re-run picked up a stale gate because I gated before editing. Same shape, one level up.

ROUND 195 - I ran the reviewer's scenario myself, got 7, and am recording it WITHOUT claiming the fix restored 8.

The reviewer's scenario was `full_body`, `exerciseCount` 8, `excludedMuscles: ['quads']`. I executed it against the REAL library (a temporary probe under `backend/__tests__/`, deleted after the run) rather than waiting for its reply, because my own number is evidence I do not have to trust:

    PROBE_COUNT=7
    PROBE_KEYS=["dumbbell_flyes","cable_crossover","straight_arm_pulldown","preacher_curl","calf_raises","hip_thrust","bird_dog"]

So the day returns SEVEN after the fix, not eight. The reviewer predicted HEAD returned 8 and the pre-fix code 7; the post-fix number is ALSO 7 on this input, and I am not going to present that as a restoration.

**What that does and does not establish.** The synthetic test settles the MECHANISM beyond doubt: a pool of exactly 7 eligible exercises across 6 families, requesting 8, returned 5 before the fix and 7 after, and 7 is the maximum that pool can supply. So the fix demonstrably stops deeper ranks being stranded. What is NOT established is whether 7 is also the real library's eligible MAXIMUM under quads-exclusion - and the two cases are very different. If the eligible pool under that exclusion is 7, the fix is complete and 7 is correct. If the pool is larger, then 7 is still a shortfall and the fix is INCOMPLETE, with some second cause at work.

I have not measured the eligible pool size, so I am leaving that open rather than guessing, and it is exactly the question I put to the reviewer: "does it now return 8 of 8, or still short? Either answer is fine - I want the executed number, not agreement." Its answer decides whether round 193 closed this or closed only part of it.

THIS IS THE DISCIPLINE THE SESSION KEEPS RELEARNING, applied to my own fix: a number that is consistent with my explanation is not the same as a number that confirms it. 7 is consistent with "the pool holds 7"; it is equally consistent with "the pool holds more and something else strands them". Only the pool size distinguishes them, and I would rather write "not established" than write "fixed" and be wrong in the register.

It FAILED before doing anything, so no database was read and nothing was written. But it is worth stating plainly what happened: **a throwaway probe I wrote to answer a question about a test fixture went looking for the production database.** The objective for this work says "no production DB", and the packet's tests avoid that by MOCKING `variationEngine.mjs` - which is exactly why the registry is only reachable through the test harness and not from a bare script. I did not intend that connection, and the failure mode was benign because the models cache was absent, but "it failed safely" is luck rather than design.

THE RULE I AM TAKING FROM IT: on this workstream, registry contents may ONLY be read through vitest with `variationEngine.mjs` mocked - the technique already used by `workoutBuilderStoredPattern.test.mjs` and `workoutBuilderTopUpReach.test.mjs`. A standalone script importing service modules is not a neutral act; some of them reach for `DATABASE_URL` at import or first call, and the packet's boundary is that production data is never touched. That belongs next to the rule-42 note, because both are about a boundary that is invisible until you cross it.

SO THE OPEN QUESTION STANDS: is the real library's eligible pool under quads-exclusion equal to 7, in which case the round-193 fix is complete and 7 is correct, or larger, in which case 7 is still a shortfall and a second cause is at work? The synthetic test settles the mechanism (5 -> 7 of 7 eligible, RED on the pre-fix bytes) and proves nothing about the real pool. ROUND 198 - I traced my own loop against the measured pool, and MY TRACE PREDICTS 8 WHERE THE EXECUTION SAYS 7. So the second cause is not where I was looking, and I am recording that rather than writing a confident second explanation.

THE TRACE. Eligible pool by family under quads-exclusion: push 19, pull 17, hinge 8, squat 5, core 9, lunge 0. Count 8 over six families gives base floor(8/6)=1 with a remainder of 2, so quotas are [2,2,1,1,1,0] for [push,pull,squat,hinge,lunge,core].
  * Pass 1: push +2, pull +2, squat +1, hinge +1, lunge +0 (empty), CORE SKIPPED because its quota is 0. Total 6 of 8.
  * Top-up, shortfall 2: push returns its top 2, both already chosen, filter empties it; pull the same; squat asked for 2 returns rank-0 and rank-1, the filter keeps rank-1 -> total 7; hinge likewise -> the trace reaches 8; and if it did not, core - never asked at all in pass 1, with NINE eligible exercises - would supply the last one.
By that reasoning the day should fill. The execution says 7. Therefore MY MODEL OF THE LOOP IS WRONG SOMEWHERE, and the honest reading is that I do not yet know where.

WHAT THIS RULES IN AND OUT. It makes the "core is skipped in pass 1" candidate much weaker than I claimed last round: core is reached in the top-up and has nine eligible exercises, so a quota of 0 in pass 1 should not by itself cost a slot. The remaining candidates are the ones DOWNSTREAM of my loop, which I have not examined at all: the shared novelty budget (its comment says it "backfills rather than shorting the day", which is now worth testing rather than quoting), and the low-impact quality gate, which can reject a family's top candidates outright.
THE NEXT STEP IS AN INSTRUMENTED PROBE, not another read: log what each family contributes in pass 1 and in every top-up iteration, and log the qualifying gate's rejections.
