ROUND 199 - BLACK-BOX DIFFERENCING ISOLATES THE SECOND CAUSE, and it is not the quota skip I suspected.

Five scenarios, same generator, only the inputs varied:

    full_body/8/no-exclusion  -> 8      (perfect)
    full_body/12/no-exclusion -> 12     (perfect)
    full_body/8/quads         -> 7      (-1)
    full_body/12/quads        -> 10     (-2)
    full_body/6/quads         -> 5      (-1)

TWO REGULARITIES, and together they name the cause.
1. WITHOUT an emptied family the day is exact at both 8 and 12. So the quota pass, the top-up loop and the `slice` are all sound in general.
2. WITH quads excluded - which empties lunge, the only family where every entry targets quads - the shortfall EXACTLY EQUALS THE EMPTY FAMILY'S QUOTA: 12 over 6 families gives [2,2,2,2,2,2] and loses 2; 8 gives [2,2,1,1,1,0] and loses 1; 6 gives [1,1,1,1,1,1] and loses 1. The pattern is the signature of a top-up pass that contributes NOTHING: pass 1 supplies the other families' quotas and the shortfall is never made up.

WHY THE TOP-UP CONTRIBUTES NOTHING, and this is the part that corrects my round-198 trace. I predicted the loop would reach deeper ranks and asked each family for the shortfall, which should hand back rank-0 AND rank-1 so the filter could keep rank-1. That reasoning assumed the families still HAD deeper ranks available. The data says otherwise: if push/pull/squat/hinge/core have already given up their entire eligible depth in pass 1, then asking again returns the same chosen candidates, the filter empties the result, and `progressed` stays false. **The binding constraint is each family's eligible depth AFTER the quality gate and the novelty budget - not the raw registry tally.** My round-197 measurement counted 58 raw eligible exercises (push 19, pull 17, hinge 8, squat 5, core 9); if the low-impact gate and the shared novelty budget cut those pools to roughly the quotas pass 1 consumes, the top-up has nothing left to find and the day is short by exactly the empty family's share.

THE NEXT MEASUREMENT, named precisely: count each family's ELIGIBLE DEPTH as the service sees it - after `applyExerciseQualityGate` and with the novelty budget's day allowance - rather than from the raw registry. That number, not the raw 58, decides whether the fix is a deeper top-up (there is depth the loop fails to reach) or an honest limit (there is no depth, and the day is legitimately short, in which case the criterion itself needs re-reading).
I am NOT writing a cause into the register on the strength of this; the differencing narrows where to look and that is all it does.

STATE: backend 10348 passed / 1247 files (`hg475`), frontend 8491 passed / 1644 files, type-check exit 0, `FRESHNESS_OK`, markdown clean, 0 stray probes.
ROUND 200 - THE SECOND CAUSE IS IDENTIFIED, and it is a re-ranking behaviour rather than a shallow pool.

THE DEPTH CURVE, quads excluded so lunge is empty, requested versus returned:

    requested  6   8  12  20  30  40  50
    returned   5   7  10  17  25  34  42
    loss       1   1   2   3   5   6   8
    lunge quota 1   1   2   3   5   6   8

The loss equals the EMPTY FAMILY'S QUOTA at every scale, all the way to 50, and the returns keep growing (42 of 58 eligible) so DEPTH IS NOT THE CONSTRAINT. Round 199's "maybe the pools are shallow" reading is now refuted by the same instrument that suggested it: there are 16 more eligible exercises the day never asks for.

THE CAUSE. `selectExercises` ranks a family's candidates from the TOP on every call. So when the top-up re-asks a family for the shortfall, it receives that family's top-N again - which are precisely the picks pass 1 already chose - the already-chosen filter discards all of them, `picked.length === 0`, `continue`, and when every family behaves that way `progressed` stays false and the loop exits with the day still short. Deeper ranks are unreachable not because they do not exist but because every call re-ranks from rank 0. This also explains why round 193's shortfall change helped the synthetic case (whose pool was exactly 6+1 and where one family's rank-1 happened to fall inside the requested window) yet changes nothing at scale: asking for MORE FROM THE TOP still returns the top.

THE FIX, identified and not yet applied: request `alreadyChosenFromThisFamily + shortfall` rather than `shortfall`, so the returned window reaches past the picks already taken and the filter can keep genuinely new ones. That requires counting how many of `chosenKeys` belong to the family in question - the loop currently has no per-family tally. It needs a RED test on the depth curve (the probe above is the shape), the implementation, the full backend suite and a freshness re-record, and this session has no budget left for that sequence, so it is recorded rather than rushed.

WHY THE REGISTER IS NOT CHANGED BY THIS ENTRY: the row already reads PARTIAL with the measurement and a candidate list. This entry replaces the CANDIDATES with a diagnosis and quotes the numbers behind it, but a diagnosis is still not a verified fix, and the row will not say more than the evidence supports.

THE SESSION'S ARC IN ONE LINE, for whoever reads this next: every one of the five explanations I wrote for this defect was wrong (rounds 145, 179, 195, 197, 198) and every correction came from running something - the quota arithmetic, the pool measurement, the differencing matrix, and now the depth curve. The depth curve is the first artifact that explained ALL the data (why no-exclusion days are exact, why the loss tracks the quota, why shortfall-based asking did not help) rather than merely fitting one observation.
ROUND 201 - THE SECOND CAUSE IS FIXED AND H19 CLAUSE 1 IS EXACT AT EVERY COUNT.

The cause identified in round 200 was that `selectExercises` ranks a family from the TOP on every call, so a re-ask returned the picks already taken, the dedupe filter discarded all of them, and deeper ranks were unreachable however deep the pool was. The fix is a per-family tally: `takenPerFamily` records what pass 1 took from each family, and the top-up now requests `alreadyTaken + shortfall` instead of `shortfall`, so the returned window reaches past the taken picks and the filter keeps genuinely new ones.

RED ON THE DEPTH SHAPE, captured before the fix: a 30-deep registry across five families with LUNGE EMPTY, asking 12, returned `expected [ 'push_0', 'push_1', 'pull_0', …(7) ] to have a length of 12 but got 10` - exactly the five non-empty quotas, losing the empty family's share. After: 12 of 12.

REAL-LIBRARY VERIFICATION, which is the number that matters:

    requested   6   8  12  20  30  40  50
    before      5   7  10  17  25  34  42
    after       6   8  12  20  30  40  50

Every count exact, unique counts matching, quads excluded so lunge is empty. The day no longer loses an empty family's share and no longer strands depth.

ROUND 202 - The reviewer was redirected at the NEW code rather than asked to judge stale bytes.

The generator review has now outlived two fixes to the file it is reviewing. Round 194 told it about fix 1; round 201 landed fix 2. What I asked of it instead is the thing I cannot do for myself: attack the TALLY for NEW defects. Four specific worries, because a fix that introduces a fresh bug is the normal shape of this workstream - the occupancy fix and the clause-1 fix both did exactly that. (1) Can `takenPerFamily` drift from reality when `takeFrom` returns fewer than requested, or when the same key arrives under two tokens? (2) Can `alreadyTaken + shortfall` over-request in a way that changes `selectExercises` behaviour - ranking, the novelty budget, the quality gate - or returns duplicates that `.slice` then truncates wrongly? (3) Is the per-family count still right after pass 1 for a family whose quota was 0 and is only ever served in the top-up, which is exactly the core family's situation at count 8? (4) Are (a)/(b)/(d) still safe, and is (c) now closed in its own reading?

That is deliberately the same calibration that has worked three times this session: one narrow question with named sub-parts and a required counterexample, and explicit permission to answer "NO DEFECT FOUND" because a false positive costs more than a miss.

ROUND 203 - Two of the four worries I sent the reviewer are ALREADY ANSWERED by my own probe data, and I should say so rather than wait to be told.

Round 202 asked the reviewer four things about the new tally. Looking again at the depth-curve probe I ran in round 201, the output was not just counts:

    PROBE_FIX requested=6  returned=6  unique=6
    PROBE_FIX requested=8  returned=8  unique=8
    ...
    PROBE_FIX requested=50 returned=50 unique=50

I printed `unique=` on every line and then did not use it. That number answers two of my own questions:
  * WORRY (2), duplicates: at every scale the unique count EQUALS the returned count, so the tally cannot be causing `.slice` to keep a duplicate - there are none to keep. The specific failure mode I was worried about (over-requesting returning an already-chosen pick that the filter somehow lets through) is excluded by data I already had.
  * WORRY (2), over-length: returned never exceeds requested at any scale from 6 to 50, so `slice(0, exerciseCount)` is holding and the tally is not inflating the day.
  * WORRY (1), tally drift from a short return: `takenPerFamily.set(moveCat, pickedQuota.length)` records what was ACTUALLY returned, not what was requested, so a family returning fewer than its quota records the smaller number by construction.
  * WORRY (3), a family with quota 0: `takenPerFamily.get(core)` is undefined for a family skipped in pass 1, and `?? 0` makes the top-up ask for `0 + shortfall`, which is exactly right.

So of the four, three are settled by evidence I already had and one - the interaction between the larger requested window and the novelty budget / quality gate RANKING - genuinely needs a mock-driven test, which is what the reviewer can do and I cannot from a black-box probe.

THE LESSON IS SMALL AND SHARP: I collected `unique=` in the same run that produced the counts, printed it, and then wrote a question to a reviewer asking about duplicates. The data was already on screen. This is the sixth instance this session of the same failure - acting on a written belief while the counter-evidence sat unread in output I had already generated - and it is the cheapest kind to fix, because the fix is only to READ THE OUTPUT I ALREADY HAVE before writing the next question.

THE HABIT THAT CLOSED THIS: naming a gap instead of filling it with an assumption. Round 204 could have written "and presumably the combined case is fine" - that sentence would have read as complete and would have been unearned. Writing "its result is unknown rather than assumed good" produced a one-call measurement in the next round. That is the cheapest possible version of the discipline this session spent twenty rounds learning: the cost of marking an unknown is one sentence, and the cost of an unmarked one is a wrong register.

TWO ITEMS REMAIN AND NEITHER CAN BE CLOSED FROM INSIDE THIS SESSION.

1. **The R-H27 bounded-grace decision.** The round-179 review measured `maxLive = 2` concurrent provider operations after a grace release with an uncancellable adapter, which contradicts R-H27's text ("retain that occupancy until settlement") while implementing the register summary's "bounded concurrency" wording. Unbounded retention and never-wedging are mutually exclusive, so one must give, and which one gives is a product decision. Three options with their costs and my recommendation (A now, C as a separate slice) are in the disposition's DECISION REQUIRED block.
2. **The generator review's formal verdict.** Its substantive question is answered - the tally fix is verified exact at counts 6 through 50 on the real library, with an empty family, with a stressed novelty budget, at a power phase, and with both stressed together - but the reviewer has not issued its verdict on the current bytes. Two of its four worries were settled by probe data and the other two by the interaction table; a formal verdict would be confirmation rather than new information, and it is recorded as outstanding rather than assumed.

WHAT THIS SESSION ACTUALLY PRODUCED, in one paragraph, because a future reader should know what they are inheriting: three hostile reviews and each found a real defect in code written by the same author in the same session - a permanent occupancy wedge that disabled the Brain for the life of the process, a bounded-grace deviation plus a test that was blind to the guard it was supposed to protect, and a short-day regression in the generator introduced by the fix that closed a previous finding. Each was found by ONE narrow question with a required counterexample. Alongside them: H19's clause 1 needed TWO fixes and five wrong explanations before the depth curve identified the cause, S04's last file was repaired, S06's integration proof was re-run against real PostgreSQL at 5/5, and four stale rows in the packet's own receipt were corrected - the last of which exposed that rule 42's pre-commit audit covers `backend/` only while the frontend carries 49 untracked and 48 modified paths, proven by an import graph rather than argued.

THE HONEST HEADLINE: my written explanations were the least reliable artifact I produced. Six times a belief I had written down was overturned by a measurement - rounds 145, 179, 195, 197, 198 and 204 - and every correction came from RUNNING something, never from rereading. The register, the ledger and the receipt were kept honest through that by one habit: when a claim was uncertain, it was labelled as uncertain in the artifact rather than softened in prose. That habit is why the packet can be handed over at all.
ROUND 207 - The R-H27 decision is sharpened by one verification: the abort signal nobody consumes.

The round-179 review said the abort is "best-effort" and that no adapter handles `signal` because the second-argument form is new in this packet. That is a claim about the whole provider surface, so I checked it rather than repeating it: a search across `backend/services` for a provider signature taking `(prompt, options)` or `(prompt, { signal })` returns exactly ONE hit - `appendSwanCoachPlanningGuidance(prompt, { placement })` in `swanCoachPlanningContextService.mjs` - which is an unrelated planning helper, not a brain adapter. The documented contract is still `async (prompt) => string`.

SO THE ABORT IS DECORATIVE TODAY. Nothing consumes the signal, and `controller.abort()` cancels nothing. That changes the shape of the decision in two ways worth recording next to the options themselves. First, the bounded GRACE PERIOD is not a convenience layered over a functioning abort - with no adapter honouring the signal, the grace is the ONLY containment that works, so option A (amend the requirement text to state the bounded grace) is the honest description of what the code can actually do rather than a weakening of R-H27. Second, option C (mandate `signal` in the provider contract and hard-fail adapters that ignore it) is not a tidy-up but a provider-INTEGRATION slice: it requires changing the contract and updating adapters that do not exist in this worktree.

That is the difference between a decision and a guess, and it cost one grep. It is also the shape of the whole session's most useful habit: when a decision depends on a claim about the codebase, spend one call checking the claim before writing the options - because three of the four options I wrote in round 184 assumed the abort did something.

Nothing changed in code. SIX ROUNDS WITH NO NEW FINDING. Rounds 202 through 207 produced measurements, corrections and documentation but nothing that changed code, and the last code change (round 201) was driven by my own probe rather than by the review. By the objective's own standard - review rounds until no new findings remain - the loop is DRY, and the honest qualifier is that it is dry by measurement rather than by a reviewer's signature. That distinction is recorded rather than smoothed, because "the reviewer approved" and "every question the reviewer raised now has an executed answer" are different claims and only the second is true.

WHAT A FORMAL VERDICT WOULD STILL ADD: an independent eye on the TALLY code as written - `takenPerFamily` bookkeeping - rather than on its behaviour. I have tested the behaviour thoroughly and read the code, and reading is the thing this session has proved least reliable about me (six overturned beliefs). So the verdict remains genuinely useful, just no longer blocking: it is recorded as outstanding and the register's H19 row rests on executed evidence, not on it.

But the receipt's open-items section - added in round 185 - still said "the generator has not finished its hostile review", which after round 208 is the stale-claim pattern this packet corrected four times: a true statement at the time it was written, kept in place after it stopped being true. The discipline is worth restating because this is the fifth instance: **a claim does not expire on its own.** The receipt, the register and the ledger each hold sentences that were accurate when written, and nothing in any of them detects that the world moved. The only defence this packet found was to re-run the command behind each numeric or status claim, which is why every row now names its log.
